The **2025 Major League Baseball season** began on March 18 (with a special two-game Tokyo Series), while the regular season ended on September 28. The [postseason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Major_League_Baseball_postseason) began on September 30. The [World Series](79597307.md) then began on October 24 and concluded on November 1 with the [Los Angeles Dodgers](18213.md) defeating the [Toronto Blue Jays](30847.md) in seven games to win their ninth title in franchise history and their eighth since moving to [Los Angeles](18110.md) in 1958.

The [95th All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game) was played on July 15 at [Truist Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truist_Park) in [Cumberland, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland,_Georgia), the home of the [Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves).[^cite_note-MLBschedule-1] The National League won the "swing-off" tiebreaker after a 6–6 tie after nine innings.

The [Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(baseball\)) relocated from [Oakland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oakland,_California) to [West Sacramento, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Sacramento,_California), for at least three seasons before their [planned relocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oakland_Athletics_relocation_to_Las_Vegas) to the [Las Vegas metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Valley).[^cite_note-Athletics-2] Beginning with this season, the team is currently branded as the Athletics, with no city name attached.[^cite_note-3]

This was the first season since [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Major_League_Baseball_season) in which there was not a no-hitter pitched.[^cite_note-4]

For the first time in Major League history, a season began and concluded beyond the borders of the [United States](3434750.md). The season opened in [Tokyo](30057.md), Japan, and concluded in [Toronto](64646.md), [Canada](5042916.md).[^cite_note-5] This was also the first time since 1964 that no team played in a fixed dome stadium, due to the [Tampa Bay Rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa_Bay_Rays) temporarily playing at the open-air [George M. Steinbrenner Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_M._Steinbrenner_Field) that season after damage to their usual home stadium, [Tropicana Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropicana_Field), due to [Hurricane Milton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Milton), as every indoor stadium that was used that season had a [retractable roof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retractable_roof).

## Schedule

Major League Baseball released its 2025 schedule on July 18, 2024. There were 162 games scheduled for all teams. The scheduling formula was modified this season to increase the number of games between "prime" Interleague rivals from four to six, playing two three-game series instead of two two-game series. May 16–18 was also designated as "Rivalry Weekend", featuring 11 series of these "prime" Interleague rivals.[^cite_note-MLBschedule-1][^cite_note-ESPNschedule-6]

The [Los Angeles Dodgers](18213.md) and the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs) opened the season at the [Tokyo Dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_Dome) in [Tokyo](30057.md) on March 18–19 in the [MLB Tokyo Series 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLB_Tokyo_Series_2025). [Opening Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opening_Day) in North America occurred on March 27 for all teams except for the [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Colorado_Rockies_season) and the [Tampa Bay Rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Tampa_Bay_Rays_season), which was rescheduled to March 28 due to work done at the Rays' temporary ballpark [Steinbrenner Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steinbrenner_Field).[^cite_note-MLBschedule-1]

The [95th All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game) was played on July 15, hosted by the [Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves) at [Truist Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truist_Park) in [Cumberland, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumberland,_Georgia). The Atlanta Braves and [Cincinnati Reds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds) were scheduled to play a [game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLB_Speedway_Classic) at [Bristol Motor Speedway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Motor_Speedway) in [Bristol, Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol,_Tennessee), on August 2. Due to inclement weather, the start time was pushed back and then the game was suspended in the bottom of the first inning. The game was completed on August 3. The [MLB Little League Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLB_Little_League_Classic) at [Bowman Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muncy_Bank_Ballpark_at_Historic_Bowman_Field) in [Williamsport, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Williamsport,_Pennsylvania), was played on August 17 with the [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners) taking on the [New York Mets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets). The postseason began on September 30.[^cite_note-7]

## Rule changes

On January 25, 2025, MLB announced a set of rule changes to take effect during the season:[^cite_note-8]

- If a team violates the previously instituted ban on the [infield shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infield_shift), which requires a minimum of two infielders on either side of second base and all four infielders positioned with both feet on the infield dirt when a pitch is thrown, the batter will be given first base, and runners will be able to advance one base. Previously, the penalty for violating the ban was an automatic ball. Teams will continue to have the ability to decline the penalty and accept the results of the play.

- Replay review will now be able to check whether or not a player overruns second or third base, and call them out for abandonment, even if that was not the subject of the original review.

## Spring training

[Spring training](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_training) for the 2025 season began in late February and lasted through most of March. Teams began workouts and practice for spring training beginning in mid-February. Pitchers and catchers reported first, followed by position players a few days later.[^cite_note-9]

Prior to the start of the regular season, each team played between 29 and 34 spring training games (with the exception of the [Los Angeles Dodgers](18213.md) and [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs), who played a shortened spring training of 23 and 24 games respectively, due to their regular season games in [Tokyo](30057.md)), beginning on February 20. There were several times during spring training when a team had two different squads playing different teams simultaneously. In addition to spring training games, teams occasionally played exhibition games with non-MLB teams, such as [Minor League Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_League_Baseball) teams, [independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_baseball_league) teams, or [college](1370306.md) teams. These exhibition games are not counted in spring training standings. Spring training ended on March 25, two days before the Opening Day.

MLB continued Spring Breakout for a second season, an initiative from [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Major_League_Baseball_season) that sees each organization put together a team of their best [prospects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prospect_\(sports\)) to compete against another organization's prospects. Spring Breakout took place from March 13 to March 16.[^cite_note-10]

### Experimental rules

During spring training, 13 stadiums were used to test the [Automated Ball-Strike System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automated_Ball-Strike_System) (ABS). When ABS was available, teams had two challenges per game, retaining successful challenges. The batter, catcher, or pitcher could challenge a ball or strike call, but had to do so immediately and without assistance from anyone else.[^cite_note-11] ABS was also used during the All-Star Game.[^cite_note-12]

### General managers

| [San Francisco Giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Giants) | [Farhan Zaidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farhan_Zaidi) | Fired | [Zack Minasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zack_Minasian) | On September 30, 2024, Zaidi was fired after six years as the team's president of baseball operations. The same day, the Giants named former catcher [Buster Posey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buster_Posey) as the new president of baseball operations.[^cite_note-16] On November 1, Posey hired [Zack Minasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zack_Minasian) to serve as general manager.[^cite_note-17] |

This will be his second stint as manager after leading the [Chicago White Sox](5945.md) in 2022 with a record of 18–16 (.529).[^cite_note-31] On October 31, Blake Butera was hired as the new permanent manager of the team, making this his first managerial position.[^cite_note-32] |  |

## Milestones

### Batters

- [Tyler O'Neill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyler_O'Neill) ([BAL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Orioles)):&#x20;

  - Hit a home run on Opening Day for the sixth consecutive season, extending his Major League record. He hit a three-run home run against the [Toronto Blue Jays](30847.md) on March 27.

    [^cite_note-34]

* [Kameron Misner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kameron_Misner) ([TB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa_Bay_Rays)):&#x20;

  - Became the first player in Major League history to hit his first home run as a walk-off on Opening Day. He accomplished this on March 28 against the [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies).

    [^cite_note-35]

- [Carson Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carson_Kelly) ([CHC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs)):&#x20;

  - Became the first player in Major League history to [hit for the cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitting_for_the_cycle) in March. He accomplished this feat on March 31 against the [Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(baseball\)).

    [^cite_note-36]

* [Aaron Judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Judge) ([NYY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees)):&#x20;

  - Became the first player in Major League history to have at least five home runs with at least 15 RBIs in his team's first six games of a season on April 3 against the [Arizona Diamondbacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Diamondbacks).

    [^cite_note-37]

  - Became the first player in Major League history to have at least six home runs and 17 RBIs in his team's first seven games of a season on April 4 against the [Pittsburgh Pirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates).

    [^cite_note-Judge1000-38]

  - He played in his 1,000th career game on April 4 against the Pirates. Judge's 321 home runs are the most for any player through his first 1,000 games in Major League history. The previous mark was held by [Ryan Howard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_Howard), who had set the mark with 279 homers in his first 1,000 games.

    [^cite_note-Judge1000-38]

  - Hit his 350th career home run on July 12 against the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs), becoming the fastest player to reach this mark. Judge accomplished this in 1,088 games, breaking the record of 1,280 games by [Mark McGwire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_McGwire).

    [^cite_note-39]

  - Hit his 20th first-inning home run on September 27 against the [Boston Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Orioles). They are the most home runs by a player in a single inning in a season. The previous mark was 18, shared by [Alex Rodriguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Rodriguez) (2001) and Judge himself (2024).

    [^cite_note-40]

  - Tied a Major League record with the most seasons with 50+ home runs when he hit his 50th home run on September 24 in the second inning against the [Chicago White Sox](5945.md). He tied with [Babe Ruth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babe_Ruth), [Mark McGwire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_McGwire), and [Sammy Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Sosa) with four separate seasons of that mark.

    [^cite_note-50_homer_club-41]

- [Eugenio Suárez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugenio_Su%C3%A1rez) ([SEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners))/([ARI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Diamondbacks)):&#x20;

  - Hit [four home runs in one game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Major_League_Baseball_single-game_home_run_leaders) against the [Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves) on April 26. He became the 19th player to accomplish this feat.

    [^cite_note-42]

* [José Ramírez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ram%C3%ADrez_\(infielder\)) ([CLE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Guardians)):&#x20;

  - Became the 24th player in Major League history with 250 career home runs and 250 career stolen bases when he stole his 250th base on May 1 against the [Minnesota Twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Twins).

    [^cite_note-43]

- [Riley Greene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riley_Greene) ([DET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Tigers)):&#x20;

  - Became the first player in Major League history to hit two home runs in the ninth inning of a game on May 2 against the [Los Angeles Angels](1360083.md). Greene lead off the ninth with his first homer of the inning against [Kenley Jansen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenley_Jansen). Later in the inning, Greene homered off [Jake Eder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Eder) to accomplish this achievement.

    [^cite_note-44]

* [Elly De La Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elly_De_La_Cruz) ([CIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds)):&#x20;

  - Became the first player in Major League history with 115 extra base hits and 115 stolen bases through his first 300 games in the modern era (since 1900) when he hit a three-run home run on May 11 against the [Houston Astros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astros).

    [^cite_note-45]

  - With his 300th career hit on May 23 against the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs), De La Cruz has reached 300 hits, 150 RBI, and 100 stolen bases for his career in fewer games than any other player since RBI became an official statistic in 1920. De La Cruz accomplished this in 310 games, breaking the record of 328 games by [Hanley Ramirez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanley_Ramirez).

    [^cite_note-46]

    [^cite_note-47]

- [Bryce Harper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryce_Harper) ([PHI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies)):&#x20;

  - His bloop single in the fifth inning against the [Pittsburgh Pirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates) on May 16, was his 1,000th career RBI. Harper became the 14th player in history to reach 1,000 RBIs, 1,000 runs, and 1,000 walks before turning 33 years old.

    [^cite_note-48]

* [Pete Crow-Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Crow-Armstrong) ([CHC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs)):&#x20;

  - Stole his 20th base of the season against the [Washington Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals) on June 3, becoming the fourth-fastest Major Leaguer in the Modern Era (since 1900) to record 15 home runs and 20 stolen bases in a season, reaching the marks in just 60 games. The three fastest were [Eric Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Davis_\(baseball\)) (40 games in 1987), [Ken Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Williams_\(baseball\)) (54 games in 1922), and [Bobby Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Bonds) (59 games in 1973).

    [^cite_note-49]

  - Hit his 20th home run of the season against the [Milwaukee Brewers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Brewers) on June 19, becoming the first player to 20 home runs and 20 stolen bases in the season, reaching the mark in 73 games. He tied for the fourth-fastest to a 20-20 showing in Major League history. The three fastest were Eric Davis (46 games in 1987), [Jose Canseco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Canseco) (68 games in 1998), and [Fernando Tatis Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Tatis_Jr.) (71 games in 2021).

    [^cite_note-50]

  - With his two home runs on July 10 against the [Minnesota Twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Twins), Crow-Armstrong became the fourth fastest player in Major League history to reach at least 25 home runs and 25 stolen bases in a season. [Eric Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Davis_\(baseball\)) did it in 69 games in 1987, [Alfonso Soriano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_Soriano) accomplished this in 91 games in 2002, and [Bobby Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Bonds) also took 91 games in 1973. Crow-Armstrong took 92 games to reach this milestone.

    [^cite_note-51]

- [Nolan Arenado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nolan_Arenado) ([STL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis_Cardinals)):&#x20;

  - Became the seventh player in Major League history to hit 350 home runs and earn 10 Gold Gloves when he hit his 350th career home run on June 19 against the [Chicago White Sox](5945.md). The other six players to achieve this feat are [Johnny Bench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnny_Bench), [Ken Griffey Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Griffey_Jr.), [Andruw Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andruw_Jones), [Al Kaline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Kaline), [Willie Mays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willie_Mays), and [Mike Schmidt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Schmidt).

    [^cite_note-52]

* [Juan Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Soto) ([NYM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets)):&#x20;

  - With his 1,000th career hit on June 19 against the [Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves), Soto became the 84th player to hit that mark at age 26 or younger. He is the 17th player to record 1,000 hits and 200 homers before his 27th birthday. He's the only player in that age bracket to record 1,000 hits, 200 homers, and 800 walks.

    [^cite_note-53]

  - With two home runs on June 26 against the Atlanta Braves, Soto passed [Jimmie Foxx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmie_Foxx) for most multi-home run games before turning 27 in Major League history. Foxx set the record during the 1934 season and had 26 multi-home run games before turning 27. Soto broke the 91-year-old record at the age of 26, eight months, and one day.

    [^cite_note-54]

  - Drew his 893rd career walk on September 23 against the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs), passing [Mickey Mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mantle) for the most in Major League history before turning 28 years old. Soto was still just 26 years old at the time. He also set the record for most walks before turning 27 earlier this season. Soto now has the most career walks before turning 20, 21, 23, 24, 25, 26, 27, and 28.

    [^cite_note-55]

- [Cal Raleigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cal_Raleigh) ([SEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners)):&#x20;

  - With two home runs, giving him 29 on the season (in 73 games), on June 20 against the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs), Raleigh passed [Johnny Bench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnny_Bench) for the most home runs by a primary catcher before the All-Star break. Bench set the record during the 1970 season and hit 28 home runs in 87 games. Raleigh broke the 55-year-old record with 22 games remaining before the All-Star game.

    [^cite_note-56]

  - Hit his 30th home run of the season on June 21 against the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs), becoming the first switch-hitter in Major League history to have 30 home runs before the All-Star break. He broke the record of 29 that he had shared with [Mickey Mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mantle), [José Ramírez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ram%C3%ADrez_\(infielder\)), and [Lance Berkman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lance_Berkman).

    [^cite_note-57]

  - Hit 33 home runs through the end of June. This total put him in a three-way tie for third place for the most home runs in Major League history through June. Raleigh tied with [Ken Griffey Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Griffey_Jr.) and [Sammy Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Sosa) as they also hit 33 home runs in 1998. [Barry Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Bonds) had the most with 39 in 2001 and [Mark McGwire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_McGwire) had 37 in 1998.

    [^cite_note-58]

  - Set the American League record with 38 home runs before the All-Star break on July 11, breaking the record held by [Reggie Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reggie_Jackson) and [Chris Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Davis_\(baseball\)).

    [^cite_note-59]

  - Became the first switch-hitter in Major League history to hit 40 home runs before the end of July on July 27.

    [^cite_note-60]

  - Hit his 49th home run of the season on August 24 against the [Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(baseball\)), setting a Major League record with the most home runs by a primary catcher in a season. He broke the record of 48 that had been held by [Salvador Perez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Perez).

    [^cite_note-61]

  - Became the second switch-hitter in Major League history to hit 50 home runs in a season, joining [Mickey Mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mantle). He also became the first player to hit 20 home runs from each side of the plate in a season. Raleigh hit the milestone home run against the [San Diego Padres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Diego_Padres) on August 25.

    [^cite_note-62]

  - Hit his 54th home run of the season on September 14 against the [Los Angeles Angels](1360083.md), tying him with [Mickey Mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mantle) for the most home runs hit by a switch-hitter in a season. It was also Raleigh's 43rd home run this season while playing catcher, passing Atlanta's [Javy Lopez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javy_Lopez) for the single-season record.

    [^cite_note-63]

  - Set a new Major League record for home runs by a switch-hitter with his 55th homer on September 16 against the [Kansas City Royals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals). The home run broke the record he shared with [Mickey Mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mantle).

    [^cite_note-64]

  - Hit his 60th home run of the season on September 24 against the [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies). Raleigh became the seventh player in Major League history with 60 home runs, joining [Sammy Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Sosa), [Mark McGwire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_McGwire), [Barry Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Bonds), [Roger Maris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Maris), [Babe Ruth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babe_Ruth), and [Aaron Judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Judge).

    [^cite_note-\:6-65]

  - Hit two home runs on September 24 against the [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies). Raleigh became the fourth player in Major League history with 11 multi-HR games, joining [Hank Greenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hank_Greenberg), [Sammy Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Sosa), and [Aaron Judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Judge).

    [^cite_note-\:6-65]

* [Christian Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Moore) ([LAA](1360083.md)):&#x20;

  - With two home runs on June 24 against the [Boston Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox), Moore became the first player in Major League history in the Expansion Era (since 1961) with multiple game-tying or go-ahead homers in the eighth or later, including a walk-off homer, in a single game within his first three career home runs.

    [^cite_note-66]

- [Freddie Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freddie_Freeman) ([LAD](18213.md)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 4,000th career total base on a home run against the [Kansas City Royals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals) in the seventh inning on June 28. He became the 91st player to achieve this feat.

    [^cite_note-67]

* [James Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Wood_\(baseball\)) ([WAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals)):&#x20;

  - Wood became the sixth player to be intentionally walked (officially tracked starting in 1955) four times in a game when this occurred on June 29 against the [Los Angeles Angels](1360083.md). Wood joins [Barry Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Bonds) (four games), [Manny Ramirez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manny_Ramirez), [Andre Dawson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andre_Dawson), [Garry Templeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garry_Templeton), and [Roger Maris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Maris).

    [^cite_note-68]

- [Manny Machado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manny_Machado) ([SD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Diego_Padres)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 2,000th career hit with a single in the fourth inning on July 7 against the [Arizona Diamondbacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Diamondbacks). Machado became the 298th player to reach this mark.

    [^cite_note-69]

* [Kyle Stowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyle_Stowers) ([MIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Marlins)):&#x20;

  - Became the first player in Major League history with five home runs in a two-game span, where one of the homers was a walk-off home run. He hit two home runs, including a walk-off homer against the [Kansas City Royals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals) on July 18.

    [^cite_note-70]

- [Shohei Ohtani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shohei_Ohtani) ([LAD](18213.md)):&#x20;

  - Became the first Japanese player in Major League history to hit a home run in four consecutive games by hitting a two-run home run in the ninth inning against the [Minnesota Twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Twins) on July 22.

    [^cite_note-71]

    &#x20;He finished with a home run in five consecutive games.

  - Became the second player in Major League history to start 100 games as a pitcher and hit at least 55 career home runs on September 23 against the [Arizona Diamondbacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Diamondbacks), joining [Babe Ruth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babe_Ruth).

    [^cite_note-72]

* [Nick Kurtz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Kurtz) ([ATH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(baseball\))):&#x20;

  - Hit [four home runs in one game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Major_League_Baseball_single-game_home_run_leaders) against the [Houston Astros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astros) on July 25. He became the 20th player and first rookie to accomplish this feat. His 19 total bases tied a Major League record that was set in 2002 by [Shawn Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawn_Green).

    [^cite_note-73]

- [Kyle Schwarber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyle_Schwarber) ([PHI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 1,000th career hit on July 25 against the [New York Yankees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees) with a home run. This was his 319th homer of his career, which is the most by any player in Major League history at the time of his 1,000th career hit. [Mark McGwire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_McGwire) had 311 home runs and [Aaron Judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Judge) had 308 home runs when they got their 1,000th career hit.

    [^cite_note-74]

  - Hit [four home runs in one game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Major_League_Baseball_single-game_home_run_leaders) against the [Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves) on August 28. He became the 21st player to accomplish this feat.

    [^cite_note-75]

  - Set a new record for most home runs hit by a left-handed hitter against a left-handed pitcher in a single season with 23 home runs against the [Miami Marlins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Marlins) on September 24. He broke the tie with [Matt Olson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Olson) (2021) and [Stan Musial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_Musial) (1949).

    [^cite_note-76]

* [Mike Trout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Trout) ([LAA](1360083.md)):&#x20;

  - Hit his 400th career home run on September 20 against the Colorado Rockies. He became the 59th player to reach that milestone and the 20th player to hit all 400 with one franchise.

    [^cite_note-77]

- [Andrew McCutchen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_McCutchen) ([PIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his tenth home run of the season on July 28 against the [San Francisco Giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Giants), becoming the 11th player in Major League history to record at least ten home runs in each of their first 17 seasons. The other ten players are [Hank Aaron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hank_Aaron), [Eddie Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Murray), [Carl Yastrzemski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Yastrzemski), [Barry Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Bonds), [Albert Pujols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Pujols), [Frank Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Robinson), [Harold Baines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Baines), [Mickey Mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mantle), [Jeff Kent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Kent), and [Willie McCovey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willie_McCovey).

    [^cite_note-78]

* [Giancarlo Stanton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giancarlo_Stanton) ([NYY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees)):&#x20;

  - Struck out for the 2,000th time in his career in the second inning on July 30 against [Zack Littell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zack_Littell) of the [Tampa Bay Rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa_Bay_Rays). He became the eighth player to reach this mark.

    [^cite_note-79]

  - Hit his 450th career home run on September 20 against the [Baltimore Orioles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Orioles). He became the 41st player to reach this mark.

    [^cite_note-80]

- [Julio Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Rodr%C3%ADguez) ([SEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners)):&#x20;

  - With his home run on August 3 against the [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\)), Rodríguez became the first player in Major League history to begin his career with four consecutive 20/20 seasons. He also became the third player in league history to reach triple-digits in both home runs and steals in his first four seasons, joining [Bobby Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Bonds) and [Darryl Strawberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darryl_Strawberry).

    [^cite_note-81]

* [Shea Langeliers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shea_Langeliers) ([ATH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(baseball\))):&#x20;

  - With his three-homer game against [Washington Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals) on August 5, Langeliers became the first player in Major League history (since at least 1900) to have three home runs in his first game hitting leadoff, the second catcher to hit three homers as a leadoff hitter ([Travis d'Arnaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travis_d'Arnaud) on July 15, 2019), the fourth catcher with multiple career three-homer games (d'Arnaud, [Gary Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Carter), and [Johnny Bench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnny_Bench)), and his 15 total bases are tied for the most by a catcher in a game (since at least 1900).

    [^cite_note-82]

- [Francisco Lindor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Lindor) ([NYM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets)):&#x20;

  - With his 20th stolen base of the season on August 16 against the [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners), Lindor became the first primary shortstop in Major League history to record five separate seasons with at least 20 home runs and 20 stolen bases. He passed [Hanley Ramírez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanley_Ram%C3%ADrez) and [Jimmy Rollins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Rollins), who each had four 20–20 seasons as a shortstop.

    [^cite_note-83]

* [Bobby Witt Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Witt_Jr.) ([KC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals)):&#x20;

  - Hit his 100th career home run against the [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\)) on August 20. He became the fourth player in Major League history to record at least 100 home runs and 100 stolen bases through his first four big league seasons, joining [Julio Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Rodr%C3%ADguez), [Darryl Strawberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darryl_Strawberry), and [Bobby Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Bonds).

    [^cite_note-84]

  - With his two-run home run on August 30 against the [Detroit Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Tigers), Witt Jr. became the first player in Major League history to hit 20 homers and steal 30 bases in each of his first four seasons.

    [^cite_note-85]

- [Patrick Bailey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Bailey) ([SF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Giants)):&#x20;

  - Became the only player in Major League history to record a walk-off inside-the-park home run and a walk-off grand slam in a season. He hit a walk-off inside-the-park home run against the [Philadelphia Phillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies) on July 8 and hit a walk-off grand slam home run against the [Los Angeles Dodgers](18213.md) on September 12.

    [^cite_note-86]

* [Salvador Perez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Perez) ([KC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals)):&#x20;

  - Hit his 300th career home run against the [Philadelphia Phillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies) on September 13. He became the eighth primary catcher in Major League history to reach 300 home runs, joining [Mike Piazza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Piazza), [Johnny Bench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnny_Bench), [Carlton Fisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlton_Fisk), [Yogi Berra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogi_Berra), [Gary Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Carter), [Iván Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iv%C3%A1n_Rodr%C3%ADguez), and [Lance Parrish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lance_Parrish).

    [^cite_note-Perez-87]

### Pitchers

- [MacKenzie Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacKenzie_Gore) ([WAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals)):&#x20;

  - With his 13 strikeouts against the [Philadelphia Phillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies) on March 27, Gore joined [Bob Gibson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Gibson) as the only pitchers in Major League history with at least 13 strikeouts and no walks in a scoreless outing on Opening Day. Gibson accomplished this feat in 1967.

    [^cite_note-88]

* [Kenley Jansen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenley_Jansen) ([LAA](1360083.md)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 450th save by closing out the victory on April 8 against the [Tampa Bay Rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa_Bay_Rays). Jansen became the fourth pitcher to achieve this feat.

    [^cite_note-89]

- [José Quintana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Quintana) ([MIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Brewers)):&#x20;

  - Became the 24th pitcher to earn a win against all the current teams on April 11 against the [Arizona Diamondbacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Diamondbacks). He joined [Gerrit Cole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrit_Cole), [Charlie Morton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Morton_\(pitcher\)), [Max Scherzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Scherzer), and [Justin Verlander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Verlander) as the only active pitchers to do so.

    [^cite_note-90]

* [Spencer Strider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spencer_Strider) ([ATL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 500th career strikeout in the fifth inning against the [Toronto Blue Jays](30847.md) on April 16. His strikeout of [Addison Barger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Addison_Barger) yielded his 500th, which made him the quickest primary starter to reach that mark. Strider accomplished this in 334 innings, breaking the record of 372 innings by [Freddy Peralta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freddy_Peralta).

    [^cite_note-91]

- [Jacob deGrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_deGrom) ([TEX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\))):&#x20;

  - His strikeout of [Jorge Polanco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Polanco) of the [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners) on May 4, was deGrom's 1,700th career strikeout. This strikeout made him the fastest pitcher to reach this mark, taking only 225 games, beating [Yu Darvish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yu_Darvish) and [Randy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Johnson), who took 230 games.

    [^cite_note-92]

  - His strikeout of [J. P. Crawford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._P._Crawford) of the [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners) on August 3 was deGrom's 1,800th career strikeout. This strikeout made him the fastest pitcher to reach this mark in both games (240) and innings pitched (1,4931⁄3). The previous records were held by [Randy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Johnson) (243 games) and [Chris Sale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Sale) (1,498 IP).

    [^cite_note-93]

* [Chris Sale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Sale) ([ATL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 2,500th strikeout in the sixth inning against the [Philadelphia Phillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies) on May 29. He struck out [Edmundo Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmundo_Sosa) and became the 40th pitcher in Major League history to reach this mark. Sale also became the fastest to reach 2,500 strikeouts in 2,026 innings, breaking the record of 2,1072⁄3 innings by [Randy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Johnson).

    [^cite_note-94]

- [Tarik Skubal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarik_Skubal) ([DET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Tigers)):&#x20;

  - Over ten outings (April 8 through May 31), Skubal posted an 89-to-3 strikeout-to-walk ratio. This is the best such ratio in Major League history over a ten-game stretch in a single season. [Cliff Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cliff_Lee) had a ten-game stretch where he had a 92-to-3 strikeout-to-walk ratio, but this occurred over a two-season stretch (September 6, 2013 to April 21, 2014.)

    [^cite_note-95]

* [Jacob Misiorowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Misiorowski) ([MIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Brewers)):&#x20;

  - Threw six no-hit innings on June 20 against the [Minnesota Twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Twins) in his second career start. This outing, combined with five no-hit innings in his debut on June 12 against the [St. Louis Cardinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis_Cardinals) gave Misiorowski an 11-inning hitless streak to open a career, which set a Major League record for the longest no-hit streak by an exclusively starting pitcher in the Modern Era (since 1900). Misiorowski is also the only pitcher since 1900 to have more wins (two) than hits allowed (one) in his first two career starts.

    [^cite_note-96]

- [Chase Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chase_Burns) ([CIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds)):&#x20;

  - Burns is the first starting pitcher in at least the Expansion Era (since 1961) to strike out his first five batters faced in his Major League debut on June 24 against the [New York Yankees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees). Burns struck out [Trent Grisham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trent_Grisham), [Ben Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Rice), [Aaron Judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Judge), [Cody Bellinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cody_Bellinger), and [Paul Goldschmidt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Goldschmidt) to achieve this feat.

    [^cite_note-97]

* [Aroldis Chapman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aroldis_Chapman) ([BOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 350th save by closing out the victory on July 1 against the [Cincinnati Reds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds). Chapman became the 14th pitcher to achieve this feat.

    [^cite_note-98]

- [Clayton Kershaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clayton_Kershaw) ([LAD](18213.md)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his [3,000th career strikeout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3,000_strikeout_club) on July 2 by fanning [Vinny Capra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinny_Capra) of the [Chicago White Sox](5945.md) in the sixth inning. Kershaw becomes the 20th player to reach this mark. Kershaw also became the fourth left-hander to achieve the feat, joining [Steve Carlton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Carlton), [Randy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Johnson), and [CC Sabathia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CC_Sabathia).

    [^cite_note-99]

* [Rich Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rich_Hill_\(pitcher\)) ([KC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals)):&#x20;

  - With his season debut for the Royals on July 22 against the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs), Hill tied [Edwin Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Jackson_\(baseball\))'s Major League record by playing for his 14th different team.

    [^cite_note-100]

- [Justin Verlander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Verlander) ([SF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Giants)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 3,500th career strikeout on August 10 by fanning [Nathaniel Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Lowe) of the [Washington Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals) in the first inning. Verlander became the 10th player to achieve this mark.

    [^cite_note-101]

* [Paul Skenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Skenes) ([PIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates)):&#x20;

  - His 50th start was on August 24 against the [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies) and he lowered his career [ERA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earned_Run_Average) to 2.02. Since the start of the Live Ball Era in 1920, the only pitcher to have a lower ERA over their first 50 starts is [Vida Blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vida_Blue), who recorded a 2.01 ERA from 1969 to 1972.

    [^cite_note-102]

  - Lowered his [ERA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earned_Run_Average) to 1.97 against the [Cincinnati Reds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds) on September 24. He became the fourth pitcher in the Live Ball Era (1920) to record an ERA that low in his age-23 season or younger, joining [Dwight Gooden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_Gooden), [Vida Blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vida_Blue), and [Dean Chance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Chance).

    [^cite_note-103]

- [Yusei Kikuchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusei_Kikuchi) ([LAA](1360083.md)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 1,000th career strikeout on September 7 by fanning [Shea Langeliers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shea_Langeliers) in the first inning. Kikuchi became the fourth Japanese-born pitcher to record 1,000 career strikeouts in Major League history. He joined [Yu Darvish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yu_Darvish), [Hideo Nomo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hideo_Nomo), and [Kenta Maeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenta_Maeda) as the only Japanese players to reach that mark in the Majors.

    [^cite_note-104]

### Miscellaneous

- [New York Yankees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees):&#x20;

  - Became the first team in Major League history to hit three home runs on the first three pitches of the game. This was accomplished on March 29 against the [Milwaukee Brewers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Brewers).

    [^cite_note-105]

  - Set a Major League record for most home runs hit, seven, through the first three innings in a game on March 29 against the Milwaukee Brewers.

    [^cite_note-106]

  - Set a Major League record for most home runs in a team's first four games of the season, hitting 18 home runs from March 27 to April 1. The previous record of 16 was set by the [Detroit Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Tigers) in 2006.

    [^cite_note-107]

  - Set a Major League record for most home runs in a team's first eight games of the season, hitting 25 home runs from March 27 to April 5. The previous record of 23 was set by the [St. Louis Cardinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis_Cardinals) in 2000.

    [^cite_note-108]

  - Became the first team in Major League history to hit three consecutive homers to start a game twice in one season. The first was on March 29 (see above) and the latest was accomplished on April 29 against the [Baltimore Orioles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Orioles).

    [^cite_note-109]

  - Tied a Major League record by hitting 14 home runs in a two-game span (August 19–20) against the [Tampa Bay Rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa_Bay_Rays), tying the mark first set by the 1999 [Cincinnati Reds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds).

    [^cite_note-110]

  - Set a Major League record for most first-inning home runs with 50 - three more than the previous mark by the 2023 Atlanta Braves.

    [^cite_note-111]

* [Los Angeles Dodgers](18213.md):&#x20;

  - Became the first team in Major League history as the defending World Series champions to begin the season 8–0.

    [^cite_note-112]

- [San Diego Padres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Diego_Padres):&#x20;

  - With their shutout on April 13 against the [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies), the Padres tied a Major League record by becoming the second team to notch six shutouts in its first 16 games of the season. The only other team to accomplish this was the 1966 [Cleveland Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Indians).

    [^cite_note-113]

* [Paul Skenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Skenes) / [Henry Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Davis_\(baseball\)) ([PIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates)):&#x20;

  - Became the first-ever [battery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battery_\(baseball\)) of No. 1 overall draft picks on April 14 against the [Washington Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals). Skenes was the first pick in 2023, while Davis was the first pick in 2021.

    [^cite_note-114]

- [Kansas City Royals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals) / [Baltimore Orioles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Orioles):&#x20;

  - In their game on May 4 in [Camden Yards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden_Yards), the teams combined for ten solo home runs, which tied the Major League record for most combined home runs in a single contest. The other times this happened in league history was on May 22, 2022 ([Diamondbacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Diamondbacks)–[Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs) at [Wrigley Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrigley_Field)) and on May 28, 1995 ([White Sox](5945.md)–[Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Tigers) at [Tiger Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiger_Stadium_\(Detroit\))). The Royals hit seven of them.

    [^cite_note-115]

* [San Francisco Giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Giants):&#x20;

  - Set a modern (1900–present) major league record set in [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Major_League_Baseball_season) for most runs scored in the 11th inning, by scoring 9 runs against the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs) on May 6.

    [^cite_note-116]

- [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies):&#x20;

  - Set a record for futility by setting a Major League record in the modern era (since 1901) for the most losses after 43 games when they lost to the [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\)) on May 14. This loss was their 36th of the season.

    [^cite_note-117]

  - With a loss to the [Philadelphia Phillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies) on May 22, Colorado fell to a record of 8–42. This is the worst 50-game start in the Major League's modern era (since 1901). Prior to 1900, the worst 50-game start in history was the 1895 Louisville Colonels, who started the season at 7–43.

    [^cite_note-118]

  - With a loss to the [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs) on May 27, the Rockies lost their 21st consecutive series, dating back to the end of last season. This set a new Major League record that was held by the [Chicago White Sox](5945.md) in 2024 with 20.

    [^cite_note-119]

    &#x20;The Rockies won their series against the [Miami Marlins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Marlins) by winning the first two games of the three-game series on June 2–3. By winning this series, the run of 22 series losses came to an end.

    [^cite_note-120]

  - Recorded their 50th loss of the season on June 1 against the [New York Mets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets). The Rockies fell to 9–50, which is the worst record through 59 games of any major league team in the modern era (since 1901). This is also the third-fastest team to the 50-loss mark, behind only the 1884 Kansas City Unions and 1876 Cincinnati Red Stockings, who got to 50 losses in 57 games.

    [^cite_note-121]

  - Set the modern Major League record (since 1901) for worst run differential, with -424. The previous record of -349 was held by the [1932 Boston Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1932_Boston_Red_Sox_season).

    [^cite_note-122]

* [Boston Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox):&#x20;

  - Became the fifth franchise in Major League history (since 1901) to record 10,000 wins in franchise history with their victory against the [Washington Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals) on July 5.

    [^cite_note-123]

- [Pittsburgh Pirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates):&#x20;

  - Tied a Major League record on July 6 against the [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners) by being involved in six straight shutouts (either winning or losing). The Pirates shut out the [St. Louis Cardinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis_Cardinals) on June 30–July 2 for three games (7–0, 1–0, 5–0) and then the Mariners returned the favor on July 4–6 (6–0, 1–0, 1–0). They became the fifth team to achieve this, joining the 1903 [Pittsburgh Pirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Pirates), the 1919 [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs), the 1949 [Chicago White Sox](5945.md), and the 1953 [Washington Senators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Senators_\(1901%E2%80%931960\)).

    [^cite_note-124]

* [Terry Francona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_Francona) ([CIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds)):&#x20;

  - Recorded his 2,000th victory as a manager when the Reds defeated the [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies) on July 13. Francona became the 13th manager in Major League history to amass this many wins.

    [^cite_note-125]

- [Philadelphia Phillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies):&#x20;

  - Became the first team since 1971 to win a game on a walk-off catcher's interference. The Phillies defeated the [Boston Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox) on July 22 when [Edmundo Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmundo_Sosa)'s bat hit [Carlos Narváez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Narv%C3%A1ez)'s glove with the bases loaded in the bottom of the 10th inning.

    [^cite_note-126]

* [Kansas City Royals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals):&#x20;

  - Tied a Major League record on July 30 by using nine total pitchers in an extra-innings shutout against the [Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves). The only other club to accomplish this was the [Cleveland Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Indians) in a 10-inning game during the 2016 season.

    [^cite_note-127]

- [Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves) / [Cincinnati Reds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds):&#x20;

  - The [MLB Speedway Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLB_Speedway_Classic) set a new Major League record for attendance in a game with 91,032. This broke the previous paid attendance record of 84,587 set September 12, 1954, when [Cleveland Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Stadium) hosted the [New York Yankees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees).

    [^cite_note-128]

* [Jen Pawol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jen_Pawol):&#x20;

  - Became the first woman to be an umpire in a regular-season Major League Baseball game on August 9 at [Truist Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truist_Park) as the [Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves) played the [Miami Marlins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Marlins). She worked both games of the doubleheader that day on the bases and worked behind the plate on August 10.

    [^cite_note-129]

- [Aaron Judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Judge) ([NYY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees)) / [Shohei Ohtani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shohei_Ohtani) ([LAD](18213.md)) / [Cal Raleigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cal_Raleigh) ([SEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners)) / [Kyle Schwarber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyle_Schwarber) ([PHI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Phillies)):&#x20;

  - Tied a Major League record by becoming the third set of four players to hit at least 50 home runs in the same season. The other seasons were in 1998 ([Mark McGwire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_McGwire) 70, [Sammy Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Sosa) 66, [Ken Griffey Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Griffey_Jr.) 56, and [Greg Vaughn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Vaughn) 50) and in 2001 ([Barry Bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Bonds) 73, [Sammy Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Sosa) 64, [Luis Gonzalez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Gonzalez_\(outfielder,_born_1967\)) 57, and [Alex Rodriguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Rodriguez) 52).

    [^cite_note-130]

* [Juan Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Soto) ([NYM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets)) / [Jazz Chisholm Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_Chisholm_Jr.) ([NYY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees)) / [José Ramírez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ram%C3%ADrez_\(third_baseman\)) ([CLE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Guardians)) / [Corbin Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corbin_Carroll) ([ARI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Diamondbacks)) / [Francisco Lindor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Lindor) ([NYM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets)) / [Pete Crow-Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Crow-Armstrong) ([CHC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs)) / [Julio Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Rodr%C3%ADguez) ([SEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners)):&#x20;

  - Set a new record for the most players (seven) to reach the [30–30 club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30%E2%80%9330_club) in a single season in Major League history. It breaks a tie with the 1987, 1996, 1997, 2011, and 2023 seasons.

    [^cite_note-131]

- [Juan Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Soto) ([NYM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets)) / [Francisco Lindor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Lindor) ([NYM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets)):&#x20;

  - Became the third set of teammates in Major League history to ever go 30–30 in the same season, joining the Mets' [Howard Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Johnson_\(baseball\)) and [Darryl Strawberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darryl_Strawberry) (1987) and the [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies)' [Dante Bichette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Bichette) and [Ellis Burks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellis_Burks) (1996)

    [^cite_note-132]

* [Cleveland Guardians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Guardians):&#x20;

  - Became the fourth team to make the postseason after having a 10-game losing streak, joining the [1951 New York Giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_New_York_Giants_\(MLB\)_season), [1982 Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Atlanta_Braves_season), and [2017 Los Angeles Dodgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Los_Angeles_Dodgers_season).

    [^cite_note-133]

  - Became the first team since at least the live-ball era (since 1920) to clinch a playoff spot with a walk-off hit-by-pitch.

    [^cite_note-134]

  - Set a Major League record after overcoming a 15+1⁄2-game deficit to win the [American League Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_League_Central), the largest such deficit to win the division (since 1969) or pennant (pre-1969). The previous record for a division title was the [1978 New York Yankees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_New_York_Yankees_season) (14 games, [American League East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_League_East)) and for the pennant was the [1914 Boston Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1914_Boston_Braves_season) (15 games, [National League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_League_\(baseball\))).

    [^cite_note-135]

    &#x20;They also set the record for largest comeback in September, erasing an 11-game deficit. The previous record of 8+1⁄2 games was held by the [1964 St. Louis Cardinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_St._Louis_Cardinals_season).

    [^cite_note-136]

- [Rafael Devers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Devers) ([SF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Giants))/([BOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox)):&#x20;

  - Became the 34th player to play in 163 or more regular season games in a single season, and the first to do so without the benefit of a [tie-breaker game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Major_League_Baseball_tie-breakers) since [Todd Zeile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Todd_Zeile) in [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Major_League_Baseball_season). Devers played his first 73 games of the season with the [Boston Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox), before getting traded to the [San Francisco Giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Giants) on June 15. Devers played his final 90 games of the season with the Giants. [Justin Morneau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Morneau) of the [Minnesota Twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Twins) was the most recent player to play in 163 regular-season games, playing his 163rd game in the [2008 AL Central tie-breaker game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_American_League_Central_tie-breaker_game) against the [Chicago White Sox](5945.md).

    [^cite_note-137]

* [Chase DeLauter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chase_DeLauter) ([CLE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Guardians)):&#x20;

  - Became the sixth player to make his Major League debut in the postseason, in Game 2 of the [American League Wild Card Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_American_League_Wild_Card_Series) against the [Detroit Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Detroit_Tigers_season).

    [^cite_note-138]

### All-MLB Team

Players were selected through fan votes (50%) and votes from a panel of experts (50%). The winners were selected based on merit, with no set number of nominees per position and no distinction between leagues.

| All-MLB Team[^cite_note-145] |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Position | First Team | Second Team |

## Uniforms

### Wholesale changes

- Major League Baseball uniforms reverted to the previous cut and materials following the [controversial Nike Vapor Premier changeover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Major_League_Baseball_uniform_controversy) in 2024. This included the return to larger letters on the player's name and pant customization options. However, the ["batterman" logo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Baseball_logo) remained below the headspoon piping on certain uniforms, as it was in 2024. For 2025, the road uniforms used the pre-2024 fabric, followed by the home uniforms in 2026. In addition, for the [2025 All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game), regular home and road team uniforms were used instead of game-specific uniforms that were introduced from [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game) to [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game). However, game-specific caps were still used. Home uniforms were also used on the [Home Run Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_Run_Derby), while batting practice-specific uniforms were still worn.

  [^cite_note-156]

- The [Guardians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Guardians) unveiled a new uniform set. The biggest change for the 2025 season was seen in Cleveland's blue alternative uniform set. The jersey continued to have a navy base but moved away from "CLEVELAND" across the chest and instead featured the Guardians' "Diamond C" logo. The Guardians' red home alternative uniform featured a new look across the chest, as the script Guardians from 2024 changed to the club's Bridge Print font with "Guardians" across the chest. Piping on the red jersey showcased a blue-white-blue look. Cleveland's white home uniform continued to carry on its primary historic look with the script "Guardians" across the chest. The logo shifted from being on a slant to horizontal. New piping was on the jersey with a red-blue-red design on the neckline and arms. A new home hat made its debut in 2025, solely with the white uniform set, as the main color of the hat changed to red with a blue bill. All hats continued to feature the "Diamond C," and the club's blue cap with red bill was worn with all other jerseys (home red, road blue, road gray). The road uniform stayed the same.

  [^cite_note-157]

- The [Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs) unveiled a new powder blue alternate uniform, featuring a stylized Cubs logo on the right chest influenced by the team's early logos, and a guitar pick-shaped sleeve patch featuring the city name. The cap was blue with a white panel, emblazoned with the stylized Cubs logo in front. The uniform was inspired by Chicago's [history of blues music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_blues) and was worn during Friday home day games in the summer.

  [^cite_note-Cubs2025Alt-158]

- The [Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(baseball\)) replaced their elephant sleeve patch with a new patch representing their temporary home of [Sacramento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento,_California), along with a silhouette of the [Tower Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower_Bridge_\(Sacramento,_California\)). The road and alternate green uniforms were emblazoned with "Athletics" in front of the city name. The Athletics also brought back the gold alternate uniform last worn in 2018, with thinner green piping.

  [^cite_note-159]

  [^cite_note-160]

  [^cite_note-161]

- The [Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox) promoted their yellow [City Connect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Connect) uniforms to full-time alternates, replacing the navy blue road alternate uniforms, to make room for a new City Connect uniform.

  [^cite_note-162]

- The [Mets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Mets) unveiled a new alternate blue pullover road jersey. It featured the "New York" script in blue trimmed with orange across the chest, which was featured on the team's road jerseys in 1987. This replaced their blue home alternate jerseys. The gray road uniforms were also tweaked, removing the blue piping and adding blue-orange-blue stripes on the sleeves, collar, and pants.

  [^cite_note-163]

  [^cite_note-164]

- The [Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals) brought back the alternate red "Curly W" uniform after a one-season absence but removed the white and blue piping. Due to the "4+1" rule by [Nike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nike,_Inc.), the team retired the white alternate pullover "Capitol W" uniform after only one season.

  [^cite_note-165]

  [^cite_note-166]

- The [Orioles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Orioles) added orange pants to pair with the orange alternate uniform for select games, and the set was worn with the orange-brimmed black "cartoon bird" cap. This marked the first time since 1972 that the Orioles would wear all-orange uniforms as an alternate option. They continued to wear white pants with the white-paneled "cartoon bird" cap for other select games with the orange uniform.

  [^cite_note-167]

- The [Royals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City_Royals) announced they would wear powder blue pants with the powder blue uniforms for Saturday home games. An alternate cap and batting helmet was also introduced with this uniform, featuring a white panel, powder blue crown, and royal blue brim, and an updated version of the original "KCR" crown logo. White pants and the royal blue "KC" cap were still worn with the powder blue uniforms on other select dates.

  [^cite_note-168]

  [^cite_note-169]

- The [Reds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Reds) added an alternate home white uniform. The design was similar to the primary home white uniform, but with the chest numbers and piping removed, and the red "wishbone C" logo replacing the full "C-REDS" logo.

  [^cite_note-170]

### Uniform advertisements

This was the third year that teams added advertisement patches to their uniforms. The following teams announced their uniform advertisements.

- [Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(baseball\)): [Las Vegas Convention and Visitors Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Convention_and_Visitors_Authority)

- [Colorado Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies): York Space Systems

- [Minnesota Twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Twins): [Securian Financial Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Securian_Financial_Group)

  [^cite_note-172]

- [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners):&#x20;

### City Connect

Main article: [City Connect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Connect)

Seven teams unveiled new [City Connect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Connect) 2.0 uniforms and wore them for the first time during the season.[^cite_note-175]

- The [Astros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astros)' second City Connect uniform was white with blue letters and featured the team nickname "STROS" in blue lettering with an orange star logo, trim carrying over from their City Connect 1.0 with their "tequila sunrise" pattern and a lunar pattern pinstriping on the pants paying tribute to the history of the moon landing. They also debuted a new hat logo, which was a futuristic design of their traditional star logo in the form of the Astros' "A". The sleeve featured a mission patch inspired by the long-time [Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Station_\(Houston\)) logo. The belt loop had "HTX" stitched along the beltline to pay tribute to [Houston, Texas](13774.md). The afterburner socks featured a mixture of orange and yellow in the design of a fire, which symbolized the afterburners on a rocket ship.

  [^cite_note-176]

- The [Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals)' second City Connect uniform was light blue featuring a white outline of [Washington, D.C.](108956.md)'s street grid and an interlocking "DC" on the chest which was meant to resemble the block "W" worn by the 1956 [Washington Senators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Senators_\(1901%E2%80%931960\)). The cap and shoulder patch featured the block "W" with an outline of the [United States Capitol dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Capitol_dome), as well as two [cherry blossoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherry_blossoms).

  [^cite_note-177]

- The [Giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Giants)' second City Connect uniform had a black base and white lettering. The jersey featured the team name in white lettering with an image of a lava lamp and was meant to flow like sound, as the letting was shaded with orange and purple hues, paying tribute to the [Fillmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fillmore_District,_San_Francisco)'s stage lights and the posters seen in the [Haight-Ashbury district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haight-Ashbury). The shading also paid tribute to the club's roots to "San Francisco's spirit of rebellion." The Giants' outfield glove appeared as a sleeve patch reimagined as a 1960s concert poster. A San Francisco mark was included above the jersey's jock tag. The cap blended the SF logo with tie-dye tones and an orange and violet gradient. A similarly colored gradient appeared on the jersey's end. A neat detail of the design was nine music genre tiles scattered throughout the socks, brim of the hat, and jersey below the tuck line, representing hip-hop, blues, hyphy, rock, country, electronic, pop, Latin, and jazz.

  [^cite_note-178]

- The [Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Rockies)' second City Connect uniform featured a split between light blue and purple, paying homage to the transition between day and night over the Rocky Mountains. The jersey featured bluebird skies and purple mountain majesty as the inspirations behind the color palette. Accents all over the uniform, cap, and branding used the red, yellow, and blue of the Colorado state flag. The Denver city flag was also featured on the lining inside the hat. It was the first pullover City Connect jersey.

  [^cite_note-179]

- The [White Sox](5945.md)' second City Connect uniform was largely influenced by the [NBA](22093.md)'s [Chicago Bulls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Bulls), another team owned by White Sox owner [Jerry Reinsdorf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Reinsdorf). The red-based design featured the block-letter "CHICAGO" wordmark, which was taken from the Bulls' black "Statement" uniform, along with black pinstripes as a nod to both teams' visual identities. On the black sleeve, an updated rendition of the "Winged Sox" logo was added, and red sleeve and pant piping featured a repeating black "SOUTHSIDE" wordmark. The uniform would be worn with two different cap designs: a red cap with black pinstripes and black brim, and a black cap with red brim. Both caps featured the "Winged Sox" logo with "CHICAGO" written inside the sock.

  [^cite_note-180]

- The [Marlins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Marlins)' second City Connect uniform had a black base, with teal and pink lettering. The jersey featured "MIAMI" in teal, which represented the past with their inaugural season in 1993, using the same color and outline in pink, which represents the city's electric glow skyline. The jersey also featured horizontal pinstripes in both the teal and pink colors with white pants that had "Retrowave" piping down the sides. The cap featured [Miami](53846.md)'s area code "[305](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Area_codes_305,_786,_and_645)" with the original Marlin logo over the top of the city area code. The brim was two-toned to represent the "Retrowave" theme, going from teal to pink. The team had two sleeve patches, one sleeve features an "[ADT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ADT_Inc.)" patch, which was a pillar sponsor of the Marlins and continues to use the "Retrowave" theme, and the other featured a patch with the original Marlin logo with the new Miami M logo. Finally, the jersey had a jock tag of the entire state of [Florida](18933066.md) with MIA on the side, which represented the Sunshine State.

  [^cite_note-181]

- The [Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox)' second City Connect uniform featured a green base, white lettering, and yellow numbers. The uniforms called "Fenway Greens" represented the colors and look of the iconic [Green Monster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Monster). The font of the uniform was the same as the numbers and letters on the Green Monster. Inside the color of the jersey featured the year "1912" which is the year Fenway Park opened, and also featured the same grey color as the concrete used to construct the Green Monster. The yellow number on the uniform represented the same color used for the score during the current inning. The jersey also featured a blue and red dot, which matched the colors of balls, strikes, and outs. The jersey had a patch with the letter "B" inside a green and white circle, which represented the city of Boston and also represented when a team gets a hit or an error on the most recent play. The hat was also the same green color as the Green Monster, featuring the normal "B" font in a grey color that matched the concrete.

  [^cite_note-182]
